recipient blood and the transplanted graft, endothelial cells that line transplanted coronary arteries may be the main antigenic trigger for the beginning and progression of CAV.

DSAs can form against the transplanted graft, especially following episodes of cellular and/or antibody-mediated rejection. Poor outcomes following heart transplantation are linked to DSA, especially to MHC type II antigens, which are related with CAV.280 Non-HLA antibodies, many of which are expressed on endothelial cells, are also probably implicated in the formation of CAV in addition to DSA. Anti-angiotensin II type I receptor, anti-MHC class I chain-related A and B, adhesion and trafficking receptors, and non-HLA antibodies are all linked to CAV. In addition, cytokine signaling and the formation of CAV may be affected by inflammatory modulators. Infections, such as CMV, may also influence the development of CAV. Significant risk factors for development of CAV are listed at Table 21.14. 280a The

long-term management of patients after a heart transplant depends on the detection and treatment of CAV. A number of other cardiovascular risk factors are crucial to long-term management (Box 21.9).

#### **Other Complications**

*Renal Dysfunction.* Early posttransplant renal impairment has a different etiology than late renal dysfunction. Pretransplant renal function281 and the complexity of the transplant are two characteristics that are connected to acute renal failure early after transplant. The development of CKD is influenced by pretransplant renal impairment brought on by heart failure, acute kidney injury brought on by hypotension, the use of vasoconstrictor drugs, and CPB, as well as hypertension, diabetes, and nephrotoxic drugs. In a setting where ischemic, diabetic, and hypertensive nephropathy are frequently present, the acute toxicities of CNI (such as vasoconstriction, toxic tubulopathy, and in certain cases thrombotic microangiopathy) might decrease renal function.

Even in the absence of vulnerable kidneys prior to heart transplant, CNI can cause irreversible alterations (such as hyaline arteriopathy, interstitial fibrosis, and focal segmental glomerulosclerosis) and progressive renal illness over the long term. CNI is therefore a significant, although not the only, cause of posttransplant CKD. The results are: (1) Depending on the etiology, patients would suffer various renal function trajectories in the early posttransplant period. For example, reversing the cardiorenal syndrome associated with heart failure may increase renal function, which may initially exceed the effects of CNI toxicity, leading to an early improvement in GFR. The initial increase in renal function is often followed by a progressive reduction in GFR (2.2-2.9 mL/min/1.73 m2 /year);282 (2) therapy of posttransplant CKD must address the several risk factors, including diabetes and hypertension; (3) reducing CNI exposure when switching to proliferation-signal inhibitors (PSI) may enhance renal function; and (4) a shorter interval between transplant and conversion is linked to greater enhancement. The existence of concurrent risk factors, however, may attenuate the improvement in GFR.119,283

Anemia is frequent after a heart transplant, particularly when it coexists with CKD. Transfusions can be reduced, and quality of life increased using erythropoiesis-stimulating drugs and iron supplements (if iron-deficient). Nevertheless, compared to a more conservative hemoglobin level (10-11 g/dL), a series of randomized trials revealed that normalizing hemoglobin levels (.13 g/dL) in predialysis patients with CKD increased mortality, cardiovascular events, and end-stage renal disease (ESRD). These findings have often led to an upper limit of hemoglobin goal of 11.5 g/dL recommended with the use of erythropoiesis-stimulating agents.

Patients with ESRD who do not have kidney transplants have poor long-term post-heart transplant survival. A single-center study found that patients having kidney transplants have longterm survival rates that are comparable to those of individuals without ESRD.284,285 In this situation, preemptive transplantation with a living donor or a deceased donor who meets extended criteria is encouraged.

#### **Management of Cardiovascular Risk After Heart Transplantation • BOX 21.9**

#### Management of Cardiovascular Risk After Heart Transplantation Diabetes

- Prevention, early detection, and appropriate therapy of diabetes should be considered as an important component of patient care after HT.
- Patients should be periodically screened for diabetes after HT by measuring fasting plasma glucose levels or with an oral glucose tolerance test (more sensitive screening test for prediabetic state) and HbA1c determination, as appropriate. The frequency of screening will depend on risk factors and immunosuppressive therapy.
- Therapies for short-term perioperative and long-term chronic glycemic control in HT recipients should be based on contemporary guidelines.
- HT recipients with diabetes should be counseled regarding weight control, diet/nutrition, and exercise; and annual screening should be performed for diabetic complications (ophthalmology, podiatry, peripheral vascular disease, etc.).
- Pre-HT risk factors should be assessed and diabetogenic immunosuppressive medications should be minimized whenever possible in HT recipients.
- CS-sparing regimens and decreased CNI doses should be used as appropriate to prevent diabetes in HT recipients.
- Hypertension should be managed aggressively in HT recipients. Annual measurements of lipids levels should be performed according to ADA recommendations.
- An endocrinology consultation may be considered when a pre-diabetic state or diabetes is diagnosed in a HT recipient.

#### Hypertension

- Anti-hypertensive therapy in HT recipients has benefits similar to those in the general population, therefore hypertension after HT should be treated to achieve the same goals recommended for the general population.
- Treatment of hypertension in HT recipients should include recommendations for lifestyle modifications including weight loss, low sodium diet, and exercise in addition to drug therapy. ACEi and calcium channel blockers may be preferred as first line therapy in patients with diabetes and as a

- CAV prevention strategy, while hydrochlorothiazide could be considered to specifically counteract CNI-induced hypertension.
- Modification of risk factors such as diabetes and hyperlipidemia are appropriate as adjunctive treatment for hypertension in HT recipients.
- Appropriate adjustment of immunosuppressive therapy, especially CS weaning, may be helpful in management of hypertension in HT recipients.
- Hypertension is common in both adults and children after HT and can be assessed with ambulatory blood pressure monitoring.
- Calcium channel blockers should be considered the antihypertensive drug of choice when optimal blood pressure control cannot be achieved with ACEi/ARB, or when these drugs are contraindicated in HT recipients.

#### Hyperlipidemia

- Biannual measurements of lipid levels should be performed in adult heart transplant recipients.
- It is reasonable to target LDL levels below 100 mg/dL (2.5 mmol/L) in heart transplant recipients, but there must be close monitoring for potential interactions between lipid lowering therapies and immunosuppressive agents.
- Proprotein convertase subtilisin-kexin type 9 (PCSK9) inhibitors and ezetimibe can be considered as adjuncts to statin therapy in heart transplant recipients with uncontrolled hyperlipidemia, or as primary therapy in those with statin intolerance. (Experience in pediatrics is limited to 12 years for PCSK9 and 10 years for ezetimibe.)

#### Aspirin

• It is reasonable to consider routine use of aspirin early after heart transplant for prevention of CAV.

Peled Y, Ducharme A, Kittleson M, et al. International Society for Heart and Lung Transplantation Guidelines for the Evaluation and Care of Cardiac Transplant Candidates-2024. *J Heart Lung Transplant*. 2024;43(10):1529-1628.e54

*ACEI,* angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitor; *ADA,* American Diabetes Association; *ARB,* angiotensin receptor blocker; *HT,* heart transplant; *LDL,* low density lipoprotein.

According to ISHLT recommendations, it's critical to continuously monitor urinary output during the first several days following transplantation. Oliguria occurs in 3% to 10% of transplant recipients in the first 24 to 48 hours following surgery as a result of the aforementioned variables. Those who were taking large doses of diuretics before transplantation owing to end-stage heart failure may be especially susceptible to this. Aggressive diuretic therapy aiming for urine production of .50 mL/h may be necessary in these oliguric patients. In administering diuretics, however, great caution must be taken to maintain intravascular volume; in cases of brisk diuresis, hourly urine output should be matched to a similar colloid infusion to maintain adequate preload (5-12 mmHg as per ISHLT guidelines).286

*Glucose Intolerance.* Diabetes and glucose intolerance are two additional risk factors that are frequently present before and after a heart transplant. Prior to developing diabetes, many patients will already have the disease. Before requesting a heart transplant, diabetes must be adequately controlled in advance of the need for steroid therapy after the transplant. Susceptible patients may develop diabetes after transplant due to the use of high dose steroids and the initiation of CNI. After receiving a transplant, between 30% and 40% of individuals will acquire diabetes. As usual, it is advised to continue screening for diabetic ocular, renal, and podiatric problems.287

*Hypertension.* Pediatric and adult heart transplant patients frequently experience hypertension, which is mostly brought on by CNI medication.263,288,289

Proposed pathways for CNI-induced hypertension include activation of the renin-angiotensin system, increased endothelin-1 synthesis, development of oxidative stress, and modification of the nitric oxide system.289,290 A recent large, randomized study of the general population showed that intensive treatment of systolic blood pressure targeting 120 mmHg resulted in improved survival when compared to standard treatment targeting 140 mmHg. This study was conducted in the general population with high cardiovascular risk.291 Although the heart transplant population was not the focus of this study, given the high prevalence of CAV in this group of patients, it is reasonable to advise aggressive hypertension treatment when other cardiovascular risk factors are also present (such as a history of ischemic cardiovascular disease, a history of smoking, hyperlipemia, obesity, diabetes etc.).

First-line therapy typically consists of angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors (ACEi), angiotensin receptor blockers (ARB), and calcium channel blockers, particularly for individuals with diabetes.292,293

*Hyperlipidemia.* Heart transplant recipients who take statins live longer and experience fewer cardiovascular events than those who do not; however, interactions with immunosuppressant drugs, particularly CNIs, can restrict dose titration or cause sensitivity to this important medicine class. Two randomized control trials demonstrated outcomes benefit of early statin therapy which has resulted in the recommended routine use of statins in management of heart transplant recipients.294,295

Recent evidence in heart transplant recipients suggests that aggressive and early low density lipoprotein (LDL) control can minimize CAV risk. Substantial data in nontransplant patients corroborate the benefits of aggressive LDL lowering in reducing residual cardiovascular disease burden.117,296 It is fair to strive for a level below 100 mg/dL (or 2.5 mmol/L) for the majority of patients, with more aggressive aims set for those with signs of CAV, even though there is no evidence for a target LDL concentration in these patients.

Inhibitors of proprotein convertase subtilisin-kexin type 9 (PCSK9) can lower serum LDL to levels previously unachievable with older lipid-lowering treatments and can lessen recurrent cardiovascular disease in persons without organ transplants.297 Several limited findings have shown that PCSK9 inhibitors can safely cut LDL in individuals who have undergone cardiac transplants without interfering with important immunosuppressant drugs.298,299 However, these series are constrained by short-term therapy, and only one study has reported angiographic follow-up, despite the fact that the early results in heart transplant patients is encouraging.300 PCSK9 inhibitors are still reasonable adjuncts to statins in adult heart transplant patients with uncontrolled hyperlipidemia or as alternative agents in the setting of statin intolerance in light of this encouraging initial data in transplant patients combined with the overwhelming safety and efficacy data in nontransplant recipients.

In nontransplant patient populations, ezetimibe can result in moderate drops in serum LDL and can lower remaining cardiovascular risk. This substance has shown promise in controlling hyperlipidemia following heart transplantation without influencing blood levels of immunosuppressive medications. As a result, ezetimibe can also be thought of as an alternative medication for statin intolerance or as an addition to statins in adult heart transplant patients with uncontrolled hyperlipidemia. Ezetimibe has not been extensively used in children with hereditary hyperlipidemias aged 10 to 17 years. Statin medication is advised following transplantation, in part to manage hyperlipidemia, as was already mentioned. High-dose, high-intensity statin therapy is avoided due to drug-drug interactions. PSI therapy has the potential to lead to hypertriglyceridemia, which may occasionally call for stopping PSI therapy. Fenofibrate and fish oil are two common medications that can be used to treat hypertriglyceridemia.117,296-300

*Osteoporosis.* Osteoporosis is a typical adverse effect of the lifelong immunosuppressive medication that HT recipients must receive, which includes CS. Although there are still some gaps in the evidence on the best pre-HT care and the function of some non-bisphosphonate-based regimens, these recommendations stress the significance of individualized management and the engagement of endocrinologists. The risk of osteoporosis after HT was decreased by a higher T score in the lumbar spine and femoral neck before HT. Although there are still gaps in the information, optimizing T score before HT should be taken into account.301,302 All patients receiving HT, whether they are adults or children, should get the recommended daily supply of calcium and vitamin D through healthy eating and age-appropriate supplementation, including weight-bearing and resistance training routines for adults and daily physical activity for kids. Within 1 year of the transplant and yearly after that, a surveillance dual x-ray absorptiometry (DXA) bone mineral density scan should be performed. Biphosphates are safe to administer in osteoporotic patients up to 3 years after transplantation, if necessary.303-305 Calcium salts (calcium carbonate 1000- 2000 mg daily) and calcitriol (10 mg/day) may also increase bone density.234,302 Osteoporosis, fractures, and osteonecrosis (also known as avascular necrosis, or AVN) are all bone problems following heart transplantation. Steroids stimulate bone resorption while decreasing bone formation. Vertebral fractures were recorded in 27% of individuals in the first 2 years following transplant in one study of patients who had yearly spine radiography.302,306

*Biliary Disease.* Biliary disease can affect as many as 8% of patients after cardiac transplantation, a prevalence 17 times higher than in the general population.307 Increased risk of cholelithiasis is explained by reduced gallbladder emptying related to vagotomy and use of cyclosporine, which is both cholestatic and cholelithogenic. Laparoscopic cholecystectomy solves this problem and is well tolerated.

#### **Cardiac Retransplantation**

Currently, 2% to 3% of transplants reported to the ISHLT registries over the past 10 years involve heart retransplantation.263,288 Retransplantation has repeatedly been identified as a risk factor for worse allograft survival compared to primary transplantation for dilated cardiomyopathy.186,308-310 Risk factors for death include early retransplantation (within 6 months of primary transplant) and retransplantation for acute rejection or early allograft failure. The incidence of infection and rejection is similar following primary transplant and retransplantation. While several focused analyses have shown that comparatively worse survival after retransplantation is primarily related to the selection of retransplant candidates with multiple comorbidities, this is especially true in the case of retransplantation for PGF, where the results are poor (see earlier section on "Primary Graft Dysfunction"). Retransplantation is now a well-established treatment for a subset of CAV patients, with results that are comparable to those of original transplantation. However, with the use of numerous therapies to halt the progression of CAV, such as the use of PSIs, drug-eluting stents, and statin therapy, the prognosis of individuals with CAV has improved over time. A recent review showed that medical therapy of patients with CAV can often offer equivalent outcomes to retransplantation.183,311 Currently, retransplantation for CAV is generally restricted to patients with severe CAV (ISHLT CAV 3) (see Box 21.8) or CAV combined with systolic dysfunction. Retransplantation is not advised as salvage therapy for severe PGD or acute rejection with severe hemodynamic compromise.

Throughout the heart transplant evaluation process, retransplantation should be discussed with juvenile heart transplant candidates and/or their parents.312 As part of a systematic plan to transition to adult heart transplant care safely, discussion and teaching on retransplantation should take place throughout the follow-up care of all pediatric recipients in a manner that is developmentally appropriate.312 Patients' and parents' fear, parents' preference for not discussing tough medical information with their children, and patients' and parents' poor recollection of previously communicated prognostic information are all obstacles to open prognostic conversation. Notwithstanding these obstacles, the majority of teenagers and young adults want to be involved in choosing their own endof-life care, before or after receiving a transplant.312,313 All prospective heart transplant candidates should be aware that risk factors specific to the patient, such as antirejection therapy adherence, renal and hepatic function, diabetes mellitus, rejection history, and the presence of anti-HLA antibodies, will be evaluated during the evaluation for retransplantation.

#### **Denervation and Cardiac Function**

Successful cardiac transplantation allows dramatic functional improvement in most patients with advanced heart failure. The cardiac allograft affords profound circulatory rehabilitation that is often immediately apparent. Yet cardiac allografts do not function normally, even though more than 90% of surviving recipients have no activity limitation.314 In the absence of recurring rejection, important allograft vasculopathy, or poorly controlled hypertension, the transplanted heart performs at rest in a similar (although not identical) manner as a healthy age- and sex-matched normal heart, but major physiologic abnormalities manifest during exercise.

Multiple factors contribute to reduced exercise reserves. Normal heart cardiectomy for transplantation severs both afferent and efferent nervous system connections. Afferent denervation affects sensory receptors in the cardiopulmonary region that exert a tonic restraining influence on sympathetic outflow to the heart and peripheral circulation.314 Absence of these afferent fibers in the transplanted heart produces a reduced augmentation of peripheral vascular resistance and blunted plasma norepinephrine response to an abrupt decrease in central venous pressure (circulating blood volume).315,316 Afferent nervous pathway interruption also impairs rennin-angiotensin-aldosterone regulation, which normally acts to decrease renin antidiuretic hormone secretion when blood volume is increased.317,318 Interruption of these afferent neural fibers results in a loss of this feedback mechanism, which normally counterbalances sympathetic renal stimulation. Chronically increased stimulation of the rennin-angiotensin-aldosterone axis creates a chronic volume-expanded state. The general decrease in vagal inhibitor effect on sympathetic output may also aggravate the hypertensive tendency after transplantation.319

Efferent innervation mediates sympathetic and parasympathetic nervous system on the heart directly. Eliminating the vagal-mediated parasympathetic influences results in a higher-than-normal resting heart rate. Loss of autonomic signaling from the CNS also attenuates the usual rapid changes in heart rate and contractility normally noted during exercise. Hypovolemia or peripheral vasodilatation in the transplanted patient results in less compensatory autonomic adrenergic effects on cardiac action. Intrinsic myocardial contractility remains unaffected by denervation, but diastolic compliance is typically somewhat impaired.320 Diastolic dysfunction early after transplantation is common, but usually resolves over time.321 Occasionally an occult restrictive hemodynamic state may persist permanently and is identifiable in 10% to 15% of long-term survivors, particularly in the presence of recurrent cellular rejection.322,323 Diastolic dysfunction during exercise, although usually not progressive, can be documented in most transplanted hearts.324 Mechanisms for increasing cardiac output in the transplanted heart allow most transplant recipients to engage in essentially all physical activities, especially after proper training.

#### **Functional Independence and Quality of Life**

In general, the first month following transplant is when maintenance immunosuppression is most severe. Infection and rejection risk are at their peak at this time, and outpatient follow-up is at its most rigorous. To help patients with drug administration, medication adherence, and transportation to numerous clinic visits, one or two caregivers are typically needed. Immunosuppressive drug target trough concentrations gradually decrease over time. Renal function and cardiac function are commonly evaluated during follow-up because decline in heart function may be asymptomatic. Prednisone and two immunosuppressants are frequently used in treatment plans. Patients may be able to gradually reduce their dosage of prednisone if they are able to avoid rejection, maintain normal left ventricular systolic function, and do not develop DSA. During cardiac rehabilitation following surgery, a patient's functional status frequently becomes better.294

Cardiac transplantation produces prompt and sustained improvement in the health status and perception of general well-being among most patients with advanced heart failure. The effect of cardiac transplantation on quality of life during the first 5 years has been examined in all four of its major domains (Table 21.15).

### **Indications for Adult Cardiac Transplantation**

In the final analysis, cardiac transplantation is advisable if the expected survival, quality of life, and functional outcomes are superior to other therapeutic options for advanced heart failure. The generally accepted indications for adult cardiac transplantation are listed in Box 21.10.

## **Special Situations and Controversies**

#### **Cardiac Xenotransplantation**

The continuing shortage of cardiac allografts has focused attention and research on using a species other than humans as a source of hearts for transplantation. Despite the initial unsuccessful attempted use of a chimpanzee heart by Hardy in 1964 (patient died within 2 hours)325 and a baboon heart by Bailey in 1984,326,327 primates as a source of donor organs was viewed as unacceptable by the public. Later, researchers considered methods of genetically modifying pigs as potential donors. In the absence of genetic modification, hearts from nonprimates are destroyed within minutes after transplantation into humans because of hyperacute rejection induced by naturally occurring antibodies against a-galactose disaccharide (Gal) carbohydrates expressed on vascular endothelium. The galactose epitope on the surface of all pig endothelial surfaces is absent in humans, apes, and baboons. The "natural" anti-Gal antibodies in humans against this galactose

#### **• BOX 21.10 Indications for Adult Cardiac Transplantation**

- 1. Heart failure (refractory low cardiac output state) requiring continuous inotropic support
- 2. NYHA classes III-IV with marked, progressive functional limitation despite optimal medical therapy and poor 12-month prognosis ( , 14 mL/kg/min or serial decrease, clinically unstable)
- 3. Recurrent or rapidly progressive heart failure unresponsive to optimal medical therapy
- 4. Severe hypertrophic or restrictive cardiomyopathy, NYHA class IV symptoms
- 5. Refractory angina pectoris despite optimal medical therapy (beta-blocker, calcium blocker, nitrates) not amenable to coronary revascularization or transmyocardial laser revascularization because of diffuse distal disease, or severe LV dysfunction. Objective documentation of myocardial ischemia within the first two stages of a standard Bruce exercise protocol
- 6. Recurrent life-threatening ventricular arrhythmia refractory to medical treatment or insertion of ICD (frequent firings or prolonged electromechanical dissociation after cardioversion)
- 7. Cardiac tumors, unresectable with low probability of metastasis

*IABP,* Intraaortic balloon pump; *ICD,* implantable cardioverter-defibrillator; *LV,* left ventricular; *NYHA,* New York Heart Association; *TAH,* total artificial heart; *VAD,* ventricular assist device; , maximal oxygen consumption.

Modified from Renlund DG, Taylor DO. Cardiac transplantation. In: Topol EJ, ed. *Textbook of Cardiovascular Medicine.* 2nd ed. Philadelphia: Lippincott Williams & Wilkins; 2002.

epitope [galactose-a(1,3)-galactose (aGal)] likely develop during the neonatal period when viruses and other microorganisms that express Gal enter through the gastrointestinal tract. In addition, humans have natural antibodies against N-glycolylneuraminic acid (Neu5Gc) and a glycan corresponding to the human Sd(a) blood group antigen (often termed b4Gal). Once anti-Gal antibodies bind to the xenograft, the complement cascade is activated, triggering endothelial cell dysfunction, platelet aggregation, and vascular thrombosis, resulting in *hyperacute rejection*. 328 In addition, there is absence of complement regulatory proteins and mechanisms to control complement activation in xenografts.

Through genetic engineering, pigs can be bred in which the three known carbohydrate "xeno-antigens" are deleted (knockout), thus avoiding immediate attack by the human immune complexes.329 In addition, up to eight additional human complement-regulatory,330,331 coagulation-regulatory, and antiinflammatory proteins have been incorporated into the pig genome. These genetic manipulations have effectively eliminated hyperacute rejection in the pig-to-primate models. McGregor, Mohiuddin, Reichart, Langin, Cleveland, and others332-335 provided initial evidence of longer-term survival (2-8 months) in orthotopic pigto-primate models using "triple knockout" genetically engineered pigs. Regulatory agencies and experienced investigators have suggested that reproducible 6-month survival of a genetically modified orthotopic pig-to-primate model would provide sufficient evidence of efficacy to warrant clinical application.336 After more than 2 decades of orderly scientific investigations using genetically engineered pigs, the experience with genetically modified pig to nonhuman primate heart transplants suggests a reasonable likelihood of short to mid-term survival in humans.

On January 7, 2022, the University of Maryland Medical Center surgical team under the direction of Drs. Bartley Griffith and Muhammad Mohiuddin performed the first pig-to-human heart transplant. After decades of research with lower species-tospecies transplantation investigations, this xenotransplantation procedure was a ground-breaking accomplishment. Ten genes were edited. Three genes were altered to block the enzymes that enable pig cells to synthesize Gal-sugars on the porcine endothelium; six human genes were added; two antiinflammatory genes; two genes that promote normal blood coagulation and prevent blood vessel damage; and two other regulatory proteins that help tamp down antibody response. A final modification removed the gene for a growth hormone receptor to reduce the chance that a pig organ, roughly matched in size to the patient's chest, will outgrow it once implanted. The patient survived for approximately 3 months, most likely limited by humoral mechanisms.337,338 On September 20, 2023, a second pig-to-human xenotransplant was performed by the team at the University of Maryland. Approximately 6 weeks following transplantation, the second human recipient of a pig xenotransplant succumbed to allograft dysfunction, likely from immune-mediated rejection. Further research into the immunological barriers of pig-to-human xenotransplantation will be required to enable this strategy to become a viable clinical alternative.

#### **SECTION II: CARDIAC TRANSPLANTATION FOR CONGENITAL HEART DISEASE AND PEDIATRIC PATIENTS**

#### **Historical Note**

On December 6, 1967, 3 days after Christiaan Barnard performed the first human-to-human heart transplant, Adrian Kantrowitz performed the second, in Brooklyn, New York, for an 18-day-old neonate with Ebstein anomaly, refractory heart failure, and previous aortopulmonary shunt for severe cyanosis.25 The donor was an anencephalic infant. The recipient died 5 hours after the transplant of heart failure and refractory acidosis. Bailey and colleagues at Loma Linda transplanted a baboon heart into a newborn infant, "Baby Fae," with hypoplastic left heart syndrome on October 26, 1984. The baby survived 20 days.326,327 This single experience renewed intense scientific interest in the possibility of xenotransplantation, while at the same time arousing intense public debate over the issue of primate donors for human transplantation. Ironically, the Baby Fae experience did not lead to another cardiac xenotransplant procedure in the coming decade, but it did signal the emergence of neonatal heart transplantation as an option for end-stage neonatal heart disease. Over the following decade, the Loma Linda group generated a landmark experience with superb survival after infant cardiac transplantation.

#### **Clinical Features**

Many clinical features noted for adult cardiac transplantation (see Section I) pertain to young patients with some exceptions. The etiologies of heart failure in the pediatric population differ considerably from the adult population, in which ischemic cardiomyopathy predominates. Among pediatric patients, .50% have some form of congenital heart disease (CHD). Among the remaining patients listed with primary cardiomyopathies, approximately 75% have dilated cardiomyopathy, 12% restrictive, 7% hypertrophic, and the rest miscellaneous types or unknown.339

#### **Neonates and Infants**

The clinical features of neonates and infants with advanced heart failure present with severe failure to thrive, tachypnea, and may show signs of low cardiac output. For some patients with complex CHD whose anatomy is not suitable for repair/palliation or if so only at high risk, cardiac transplantation may be the selected therapy (see later section on "Indications for Operation"). In this setting, the patient may show typical clinical signs and symptoms consistent with the uncorrected or partially palliated malformation, without evidence of overt decompensation. The areas where clinical features differ importantly from the adult population are discussed later.

#### **Failing Fontan**

Fontan failure is generally caused by elevations in pulmonary vascular resistance or systemic ventricular systolic or diastolic function, which elevate Fontan circuit pressure in the absence of a pump in the pulmonary circulation (see also Chapter 52). Based on an analysis of failing Fontan patients from 16 centers, Dykes and colleagues described four basic Fontan phenotypes that account for the pathophysiologic manifestations of Fontan failure: Fontan with abnormal lymphatics (47%), Fontan with reduced systolic function (33%), Fontan with normal ventricular function by echo (12%), and Fontan with preserved systolic function but often diastolic dysfunction (8%).340,341 Often more than one of these phenotypes are present.

Patients with a *failing Fontan* usually do not have typical signs or symptoms of heart failure. Less than 10% of pediatric patients with failing Fontan listed for heart transplantation exhibit tachypnea, orthopnea, gallop rhythm, or jugular venous distension.341 Also uncommon are growth failure, nausea, and other typical findings associated with pediatric heart failure. Instead, protein losing enteropathy (40%) or plastic bronchitis with preserved ventricular function, systolic dysfunction with few symptoms (30%), and isolated signs of right heart failure such as hepatomegaly (65%) with ascites (35%) are more common. Manifestations of elevated Fontan pressures are much more common than signs of low cardiac output.

#### **Donor Selection in Pediatric Patients**

The details of donor evaluation and selection are covered in detail in "Donor Evaluation and Allocation" in Section I. In matching of donor and recipient by size, several studies indicate that other parameters than weight may provide a better indicator of suitable size matching in the pediatric population. One attractive alternative is measurement of total cardiac volume (TCV).342 Although modalities such as computed tomography are available for measurement in most potential recipients, few donors would have the possibility of such measurements. However, specified views of the heart on chest radiogram or echocardiography may allow reasonably accurate derivation of TCV.343

#### **Waitlist Outcomes**

Pediatric patients listed for heart transplantation receive organs according to a priority algorithm established within each country or region. In the United States, the algorithm is established by UNOS. Waitlist mortality has progressively declined over the past 3 decades, both for cardiomyopathy (Fig. 21.23) and CHD (Fig. 21.24).339 Survival at 1 year has improved from about 70% in the early 2000s to .80% between 2020 and 2019. The largest improvement has occurred in the highest priority patients.339

#### **Socioeconomic Factors**

A multiinstitutional study by Amdani and colleagues found that children from distressed communities (reflecting lower community socioeconomic well-being), irrespective of race, were more likely than their counterparts from more prosperous communities to be listed with more advanced heart failure in the most urgent status or on MCS.<sup>344</sup> These differences in severity of heart failure at listing suggest the potential for bias based on socioeconomic factors. In at least one study, formal evaluation of the referral and evaluation processes did not reveal racial disparities in access to listing.<sup>345</sup>

Patients with CHD are known to be at increased risk for mortality on the waitlist compared to children with cardiomyopathy. 346 Several specific cohorts merit special mention.

![](_page_49_Figure_10.jpeg)

• Figure 21.25 Survival after listing for single ventricle patients, stratified by stage of palliation. PHTS 2010–2020; n=1937. (From Richmond ME, Conway J, Kirklin JK, et al. Three decades of collaboration through the Pediatric Heart Transplant Society Registry: a journey through registry data with a highlight on children with single ventricle anatomy. *Pediatr Transplant*. 2024;28:e14615.)

#### **Functional Single Ventricle**

Stage of palliation in patients with single ventricle affects likelihood of death while awaiting transplantation (Fig. 21.25). Patients (usually infants) before or after stage 1 palliation have a high waitlist mortality, approaching 40% if transplant cannot be performed within 3 to 6 months.<sup>339</sup>

#### **Failing Fontan**

Overall waitlist mortality for failing Fontan patients is about 10% to 14%. 341 Higher Fontan pressure is associated with increased risk of death on the waitlist, especially among patients with reduced ventricular function, likely related to associated multiorgan dysfunction. Of interest, plastic bronchitis with or without other manifestations of protein-losing enteropathy, is not associated with higher waitlist mortality. 347

## Pulmonary Atresia with Intact Ventricular Septum

Among patients with single ventricle physiology, patients with pulmonary atresia and intact ventricular septum (PAIVS) have a more favorable survival on the waitlist, which is supported by an analysis from the Pediatric Heart Transplant Society (PHTS) that identified PAIVS as protective compared to other single ventricle cohorts for waitlist survival (HR 0.68, P=.03). <sup>348</sup> A prior aortopulmonary shunt was a favorable factor for waitlist survival among PAIVS patients.

#### Myocarditis

Children with myocarditis are generally sicker at the time of listing compared to patients with dilated cardiomyopathy. A multicenter study from PHTS found that patients with myocarditis (representing 3% of over 9000 listed pediatric patients between 1993 and 2019) were listed at higher urgency level and more frequently in an intensive care unit, intubated, and sup-

ported on ECMO or MCS compared to children listed with dilated cardiomyopathy.349 The increased risk of death on the waitlist in these patients is explained by their generally higher acuity of illness.

#### **Mechanical Circulatory Support**

MCS as a bridge-to-transplant therapy generally involves the options of ECMO, pulsatile or continuous flow extracorporeal devices in infants and children, and continuous flow intracorporeal devices in older children and adolescents (see detailed descriptions in Chapter 20, "Mechanical Circulatory Support"). Bridging with ECMO presents a high risk for mortality, especially among infants. Among infants ,10 kg, about 5% to 8% of those on the waitlist receive ECMO support while waiting, with no difference between CHD and cardiomyopathy patients.350 Among infants with CHD, VAD support is less common (7%) than among those with cardiomyopathy (30%) (*P* , .001). Survival at 3 months is generally better with VAD support than with ECMO (74% vs. 49%, *P* , .001), but among small infants ,5 kg, survival is similarly poor with both modalities, especially in patients with CHD. Currently, .25% of pediatric transplant recipients are on VAD support at transplant.339

#### **Risk Factors for Waitlist Mortality**

A multicenter analysis of North American centers identified these risk factors for mortality on the waitlist: weight ,10 kg (OR 2.7), diagnosis of CHD (OR 2.4), blood type O (OR 2.2), ECMO (OR 1.5), mechanical ventilation (OR 1.8), and renal dysfunction (OR 1.6).351

### **Technique of Operation**

For certain forms of CHD, special adaptations of basic techniques of transplantation discussed in Section I are necessary to accommodate anatomic variations. These are described separately. If the bicaval technique is used, it is especially important to avoid excessive redundancy in creating the new SVC because any kinking of the caval pathway can result in a venous pressure gradient. In small infants and children, a beveled anastomosis is recommended to minimize narrowing at the suture line with growth. In general, mean systemic perfusion pressure should be maintained at 40 mmHg or greater during rewarming after heart transplantation with a perfusion flow rate of 2.5 to 3.0 L·min<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> ·m<sup>2</sup><sup>2</sup> in pediatric patients.

#### **Venous Cannulation**

In the presence of multiple previous operations and difficult venous cannulation, some surgeons prefer to use initial cannulation of the right atrium and subsequent direct venous cannulation while cooling, particularly if a left SVC is present. Individual caval cannulation eliminates the need for prolonged periods of circulatory arrest during transplantation. In patients with separate hepatic drainage, these veins are allowed to drain directly into the pericardium for collection by a cardiotomy sucker. Care must be taken to avoid obstructing hepatic venous drainage by a snare around the IVC. In patients with heterotaxy syndrome and azygos continuation of the IVC, the right and left hepatic veins often drain separately, and it is important to leave a cuff of atrium around the entrance of the hepatic veins to facilitate connection of the right atrium to the donor graft.

#### **Arterial Cannulation**

In neonates with hypoplastic left heart syndrome prior to stage 1 palliation, it is attractive to use a 3.5-mm polytetrafluoroethylene (PTFE) graft sewn onto the brachiocephalic artery. This permits regional cerebral perfusion while implanting the donor aorta, avoiding periods of circulatory arrest. A flexible wire-reinforced cannula is desirable for arterial cannulation whenever arch reconstruction is required, because the cannula can be positioned away from the operative field (see "Arterial Cannulation" in Chapter 2).

#### **Malpositions of the Aorta**

In patients with a malposed ascending aorta or transposed great arteries, the ascending aorta may be distinctly leftward and relatively short compared with a normal dextropositioned aorta. The pulmonary trunk is often posterior and rightward. However, at the pericardial reflection the aorta usually assumes a position anterior and to the right of the pulmonary trunk. These malpositions are most easily addressed by harvesting from the donor an extended length of the aorta including the arch and pulmonary artery including the bifurcation, and in the recipient extensively separating and mobilizing the ascending aorta from the pulmonary trunk. This allows a gentle arc of donor ascending aorta to overlie the pulmonary trunk reconstruction, with an anastomosis near the native brachiocephalic artery. The native pulmonary trunk can be partially or completely oversewn and an incision extended into the left pulmonary artery to create an anatomic position suitable for connection with the donor pulmonary trunk.

#### **Anomalies of Systemic Venous Return**

*Left Superior Vena Cava.* When the left SVC drains to the coronary sinus, the simplest approach is to leave it attached to the atrium and preserve the course and connection of the coronary sinus to the right atrium at cardiectomy (Fig. 21.26). If a biatrial transplant is selected, care must be taken to avoid narrowing the coronary sinus by the left atrial anastomosis. In the presence of a diminutive or absent right-sided SVC, this method of reconstruction may prevent EMB via the left SVC because of the sharp angulation required for the bioptome to access the right ventricular septum.

If the left SVC empties directly into the upper corner of the left atrium, and if there is a communicating vein to the right SVC, consideration should be given to ligating it at its entry into the left atrium. However, in general it is advisable to preserve all avenues of venous drainage. To maximize surgical options during implantation, the maximal length of donor SVC and brachiocephalic vein should be harvested. An extracardiac reconstruction can be accomplished by anastomosing the donor brachiocephalic vein to the cut end of the recipient left SVC, followed by a direct right SVC anastomosis to donor right SVC (Fig. 21.27).

Alternatively, an intracardiac pathway can be constructed using the recipient's left atrial appendage or PTFE to redirect drainage from the left SVC to the right atrium. The biatrial implantation technique is then employed, in which the left atrial and right atrial septal anastomoses deviate slightly leftward at the opening of the left SVC tunnel into the new right atrium. This technique may be less prone to kinking and thrombosis than the extracardiac reconstruction (Fig. 21.28).

*Anomalies of Inferior Vena Caval Drainage.* Anomalies of systemic venous drainage are common. Separate drainage of the

hepatic veins to the left atrium requires creating a baffle within the left atrium to divert left hepatic drainage back to the right atrium (Fig. 21.29). #### **Anomalies of Pulmonary Veins and Atrial Septum**

In the presence of uncorrected partial or total anomalous pulmonary venous connection, standard transplant procedures must be modified (see Section I). In the presence of anomalous connections of the right upper pulmonary veins to the SVC, a recommended option is to use a modification of the Warden procedure, in which the SVC is divided and oversewn just cephalad to the pulmonary veins, and the septum is excised along with the explanted heart. The left atrial incision is fashioned to include the right atrial wall at its junction with the SVC. The entrance of the anomalous pulmonary vein is then incorporated with the left atrial anastomosis. The donor SVC is then anastomosed end to end to the recipient's SVC.

In the presence of previous Mustard or Senning procedures (see Chapter 52), the entire interior baffle is removed and the interatrial septum reconstructed with PTFE in preparation for a biatrial connection. Alternatively, the venae cavae can be divided at their connection to the native heart and a bicaval implantation performed.

#### **Complex Combinations of Anomalous Systemic and Pulmonary Venous Drainage**

In the setting of multiple previous operations and unexpected anomalies of pulmonary or systemic venous return, little or no native tissue may be available for reconstruction. Any anomalous locations of entry into the atrial mass can be managed expeditiously by cutting the ventricular mass off the underlying atria, leaving all available atrial tissue in place while the surgeon assesses reconstructive options. Any interatrial septum is excised, leaving a "common atrium." Any systemic venous opening to the left of the midportion of the open atria can be moved to the midline and repartitioned or tunneled with PTFE patch material to drain to the right side. Any pulmonary veins entering to the right of a midatrial (interatrial) septum can be repartitioned with a patch of PTFE that replaces part or all of the septum, such that there is a cut edge of PTFE partition (septum) near the midportion of the atria. The pulmonary veins drain under this partition to the left side of the atria. Then, a standard orthotopic implantation method can be used in which a newly created edge of atrial "septum" is available for the left and right atrial "septal" suture lines, such that all pulmonary venous blood drains to the left side, and all systemic venous blood drains to the right side.

#### **Dextrocardia**

In patients with isolated dextrocardia, in which the apex of the ventricles is rightward with atrial situs solitus, orthotopic cardiac transplantation can usually be performed in standard fashion. There may be rotation of the great vessels, but extensive mobilization of the aorta and pulmonary trunk allows rather standard anastomoses for the transplanted heart. Occasionally, secondary to limited space available on the left side of the pericardium, the left pleural space can be widely opened and the diaphragmatic pericardial attachments taken down partially on the left side to accommodate the apex of the transplanted heart.

#### **Situs Inversus**

Situs inversus is a unique surgical challenge because of the need to reroute systemic and pulmonary pathways and reposition the great vessels. Management of the great vessels is accomplished as discussed under "Malpositions of the Aorta" earlier in this section. Reconstruction of the systemic venous and pulmonary venous pathways can be accomplished using either the biatrial or bicaval technique.

The simplest method uses a bicaval technique in which the pulmonary vein orifices and the posterior aspect of the left atrium are isolated as a single unit with a circumferential cuff of left atrium, just as in a standard bicaval technique. Incisions in the left-sided right atrium are planned such that autologous right atrial tissue can be used to construct a partial tube from the leftsided opening of the IVC rightward along the diaphragm to provide a connection with the donor IVC opening within the right atrium.

In the presence of a normal brachiocephalic vein, a long segment of donor SVC and brachiocephalic vein is harvested to allow a right-sided anastomosis from donor SVC to the underside of the recipient brachiocephalic vein. Alternatively, the donor SVC and brachiocephalic vein can be anastomosed directly to the mobilized recipient left SVC (Fig. 21.30).

The general sequence of operation is transection of the SVC, including a small rim of right atrium, followed by incision with preservation of all available right atrial tissue anteriorly to the tricuspid valve and posteriorly to the interatrial septum, which is left intact and in continuity with the IVC orifice. The great vessels are divided proximally, and the left atrium is incised and the cardiac mass excised, leaving a cuff of left atrium around all the pulmonary venous orifices. The great vessels are mobilized as described earlier. Native atrial tissue is used to construct a partial tube (using a Hegar dilator if needed) for the IVC that reaches beyond the lower right corner of the left atrial remnant. If insufficient pericardial space is available for the donor ventricular apex, the pericardial attachments to the diaphragm on the left side are partially taken down, avoiding injury to, or cautery close to, the phrenic nerve.

![](_page_52_Figure_14.jpeg)

**• Figure 21.30** Final appearance of implantation of a situs solitus donor heart into a situs inversus recipient. Ascending aortic anastomosis is shortened, allowing superior venous drainage system to be draped over the great arteries. *BV,* Brachiocephalic vein; *LIVC*, left inferior vena cava; *LSVC*, left superior vena cava; *SVC*, superior vena cava. (Courtesy Leonard L. Bailey, MD, Loma Linda, Calif.; del Nido PJ, Bailey LL, Kirklin JK. *Surgical Techniques in Pediatric Heart Transplantation*. In: Canter CE, Kirklin JK, eds. Pediatric Heart Transplantation. Phildelphia: Elsevier: 2007:83-102.)

Implantation involves a standard left atrial anastomosis followed by the IVC anastomosis between the constructed tube and the donor IVC orifice. The donor and recipient aorta are then trimmed appropriately to provide a tension-free anastomosis, after which de-airing and de-clamping are carried out as usual. The SVC reconstruction and pulmonary artery anastomosis (with an incision extended onto the left pulmonary artery as needed) are performed while rewarming.

Alternatively, a biatrial technique can be used by removing all interatrial septum and reconstructing the septum as described earlier in this section under "Complex Combinations of Anomalous Systemic and Pulmonary Venous Drainage*.*" Autologous tissues can also be used to create internal baffles to achieve the same result (Fig. 21.31).

#### **Pulmonary Arterial Anomalies**

Distortion or hypoplasia of the central pulmonary arteries must be repaired at the time of transplantation to minimize obstruction to right ventricular outflow. Repair can usually be made by patch plasty with donor pulmonary artery tissue or by replacing the central pulmonary arteries with those of the donor. In more severe forms of pulmonary arterial anomalies, discontinuity of pulmonary arteries may occur because of previous systemic-to-pulmonary connections or cavopulmonary artery connections, or they may be congenitally discontinuous. In these instances, detailed preoperative angiography or computed tomographic angiography is important to delineate the size and distribution of the pulmonary arteries, extent and location of major aortopulmonary collateral arteries, and those specific areas of the pulmonary artery tree that will require reconstruction (see Chapter 35).

Management of aortopulmonary collaterals during CPB requires special planning. Because these collaterals markedly increase pulmonary venous return during bypass, they can complicate the conduct of the transplant procedure and produce important volume overload and distension of the new graft during reperfusion. In addition, excessive pulmonary blood flow may initiate pulmonary dysfunction, pulmonary hypertension, and right ventricular dysfunction. For these reasons, coiling these vessels in the catheterization laboratory immediately prior to transplantation is preferable whenever possible. Alternatively, ligation of collaterals at transplantation may be accomplished by dividing the posterior pericardium following cardiectomy (see "Managing Sources of Pulmonary Blood Flow on Cardiopulmonary Bypass" in Chapter 35).

When the pulmonary trunk requires replacement, it is advisable to perform this reconstruction before the aortic or SVC anastomosis (Fig. 21.32). After the left atrial connection is completed, the donor pulmonary arteries are connected to the recipient's pulmonary arteries, after which the aortic and cavocaval or right atrial anastomoses are completed.

#### **Anomalies of Aortic Arch**

Residual or congenital defects of the aorta are often encountered, along with arch hypoplasia, interruption, or hypoplastic left heart syndrome. In patients with aortic arch hypoplasia, such as in hypoplastic left heart syndrome, the donor aorta can be used to replace a small ascending aorta and augment a hypoplastic aortic arch. In such instances, it is important to obtain as much of the donor aortic arch as possible, preferentially well into the descending thoracic aorta.

In the presence of residual coarctation, reconstruction optimally involves resecting the coarctation segment and anastomosis of the back wall of the transverse aortic arch to the descending aorta, followed by anterior augmentation with the donor aorta. This provides the greatest freedom from late re-coarctation. In the setting of ascending aorta and arch replacement, it is surgically convenient to leave a stump of donor brachiocephalic artery available for arterial recannulation following implantation of the donor heart (unless initial cannulation was in the native brachiocephalic artery via a PTFE graft).

#### **Hypoplastic Left Heart Syndrome**

Patients presenting with hypoplastic left heart physiology without prior palliation may have a spectrum of anomalies. Common to all, however, is hypoplasia of the left ventricle and ascending aorta (Fig. 21.33). The aortic arch is also small in most cases, and there may be associated coarctation of the aorta. The right ventricle and pulmonary trunk are usually large, and the ductus arteriosus continues to the descending aorta. It must be identified relative to the

Although a major portion of the transplant operation for this condition has traditionally been performed during circulatory arrest, alternative strategies are available to minimize or eliminate circulatory arrest to reduce the potential for neurologic damage. Prior to CPB, a 3.5-mm PTFE graft is anastomosed to the brachiocephalic artery and clamped. The patient is heparinized, and CPB is initially established with the arterial cannula inserted into the PTFE tube and secured with silk ties (this is a standard technique used by some surgeons for first-stage palliation of hypoplastic left heart syndrome; see "Technique of Operation" in Chapter 51). A second arterial cannula is inserted into the distal pulmonary trunk and passed well into the ductus arteriosus for distal aortic perfusion. Upon initiating CPB, a snare is secured around the ductus to exclude the pulmonary arteries from arterial perfusate inflow.

Bicaval venous cannulation is employed as for the standard biatrial implantation technique, although initial cannulation of the right atrium may be employed if there is hemodynamic instability prior to initiating CPB. The perfusate is cooled to bring the nasopharyngeal temperature to 18°C to 20°C. During the cooling phase, the hypoplastic ascending aorta is clamped and the heart excised as for biatrial implantation. Once the target body temperatures are achieved, the perfusate temperature is set at 20°C and low-flow perfusion is established as needed for visualization at 1.0 to 1.5 L·min<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> ·m<sup>2</sup><sup>2</sup> for the remainder of the implantation.

The donor heart has been procured to include the entire aortic arch and some of the descending thoracic aorta. The aortic arch of the donor is tailored by excising approximately one fourth of the superior aspect of the aortic circumference, beginning distal to the take-off of the donor brachiocephalic artery. The length of donor aortic arch is then tailored to fit the aortic arch of the recipient.

The left atrial anastomosis is constructed with continuous 5-0 polypropylene suture as in the standard biatrial implantation. The perfusate flow is then reduced to about 0.5 L ·min<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> ·m<sup>2</sup>2 and the recipient brachiocephalic artery clamped proximal to the PTFE graft to allow continuation of regional cerebral perfusion during arch implantation. The arterial cannula through the ductus is clamped and removed, and the ductus is ligated proximally and divided. Inferior traction on this ligature facilitates the arch reconstruction. The native aorta is divided near the brachiocephalic artery and the proximal ascending aorta remnant removed. The aortic arch is opened through its inferior aspect, extending the incision past the ductus into the upper portion of the descending thoracic aorta to encompass any coarcted segment. All ductal tissue in the upper descending thoracic aorta is removed.

The donor aortic arch is anastomosed to the recipient aortic arch with continuous 6-0 polypropylene suture. The most difficult portion of the repair is the anastomosis of the distal aortic arch of the donor to the apex of the aortotomy in the upper portion of the descending thoracic aorta of the recipient. Accuracy and complete hemostasis in this area are critical for two reasons. First, inclusion of residual ductal tissue or failure to extend the incision far enough distally could result in stenosis and recurrent coarctation; second, bleeding from this area can be difficult to control later. Exposure of this area is facilitated by placing a Cclamp on the distal aorta to prevent back bleeding, which can obscure the surgical field. Upon completion of the aortic reconstruction, CPB can be continued through the native brachiocephalic artery cannulation, or a cannula can be placed through the stump of the donor brachiocephalic artery for the remainder of the procedure. After de-airing the arch and heart, the suture lines are secured, and the aortic clamp is removed while suction is placed on the needle vent. During rewarming, the right atrial anastomosis and pulmonary trunk end-to-end anastomoses are constructed using 5-0 polypropylene suture.

#### **Transplant after Fontan Operation**

These patients are often very ill, with ascites, malnutrition, and marked venous hypertension. Multiple prior sternotomies are the norm, and specific plans are essential for establishing CPB rapidly in the event of cardiac bleeding during sternotomy (see Chapter 5). Exposure of a femoral artery and vein is advisable if the patient is large enough for femoral cannulation. If percutaneous femoral cannulae are planned in the event of an emergency, the groin should be inspected for scars of previous cutdowns or multiple catheterization procedures. If scarring is present, surgical exposure of the femoral artery and vein is advisable, because passage of dilators over a guidewire in a previously operated groin can be difficult.

The bicaval technique is employed with direct caval venous cannulation. The SVC is detached from the pulmonary artery (prior bidirectional Glenn), and the right atrial or IVC connection to the pulmonary trunk or underside of the right pulmonary artery is disconnected. Defects in the pulmonary artery are preferably reconstructed with donor pulmonary artery tissue or thin bovine pericardium. Following this reconstruction, maximal hemostasis should be obtained before bringing the donor heart onto the surgical field for implantation.

The reconstructive requirements are often such that maximal length of SVC and brachiocephalic vein (if a left SVC is present) should be harvested from the donor, along with sufficient length of pulmonary artery branches to meet the reconstructive needs of the recipient pulmonary arteries.

## **Special Features of Postoperative Care**

Postoperative care follows the same general principles as for all infants and children undergoing open cardiac operations. For infants undergoing cardiac transplantation, assessment of allograft function with transthoracic echocardiography is usually performed daily for the first several days, particularly as inotropic support is being weaned. Thereafter, echocardiographic assessment of ventricular and valvar function occurs at least biweekly during the hospitalization because this is the major modality for detecting signs of rejection when endomyocardial biopsies are not routinely performed.

The hospital length of stay following pediatric heart transplantation averages about 2 1/2 to 3 weeks but is highly dependent on patient-related characteristics. In a study by Gupta and colleagues from the PHTS, the overall median length of stay (LOS) was 19 days but longer in patients with CHD (25 days) than with cardiomyopathy (17 days) (*P* , .05).352 Age was highly correlated with LOS, with patients ,1 year of age having longer median LOS (26 days) compared to patients .10 years (16 days) (*P* , .05). Other factors associated with a prolonged LOS included previous cardiac surgery, single ventricle physiology, recipient history of cardiac arrest, renal dysfunction, ventilated at time of transplant, and urgent status at transplant.

#### **Dosage of Immunosuppression Agents in Pediatric Patients • BOX 21.11**

#### Corticosteroids

For neonates and small infants (,7 kg), generally 100 mg methylprednisolone IV daily for 3 days. For older infants and children, methylprednisolone 15 mg·kg<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> IV daily 3 3 or prednisolone 2-4 mg·kg<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> daily PO 3 3 with or without a subsequent taper

#### Cyclosporine

Initial oral dose of about 0.3 mg·kg<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> ·day<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> twice daily. If renal function stable, gradually increase to oral dose of about 1 mg·kg<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> ·day<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> in 3 divided doses. If renal function remains normal, rapidly increase over 3-4 days to achieve target trough levels

#### Tacrolimus

0.05 to 0.15 mg · kg<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> · day<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> PO or sublingual in two divided doses. Once stable renal function is verified after initial 1-2 days of therapy, dosage can be rapidly increased to attain target level (with continued surveillance of renal function)

#### Azathioprine

2 to 2.5 mg · kg<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> · day<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> . Dose reduced as necessary to maintain the white blood cell count of 3000 per mL or greater

#### Mycophenolate Mofetil

Begin at 5 mg · kg<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> IV or twice daily PO and progressively increase to a target dose of 20-23 mg · kg<sup>2</sup>1 twice daily if tolerated (gastrointestinal symptoms). When administered IV, dose is equivalent to the oral dose

#### Cyclophosphamide

1 to 1.5 mg · kg<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> · day<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> . Dose reduced as necessary to maintain the white blood cell count of 3000 per mL or greater

#### Methotrexate

5 to 10 mg ·m<sup>2</sup><sup>2</sup> ·week<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup> in 3 every-12-hour doses

#### Sirolimus

Loading dose: 3 mg ·m<sup>2</sup><sup>2</sup> followed by Maintenance dose: 1 mg ·m<sup>2</sup><sup>2</sup> · day<sup>2</sup><sup>1</sup>

#### Antithymocyte Globulin

Specifics regarding dosage, premedications, and hypersensitivity testing vary according to specific antithymocyte preparation

#### Anti-CD25 Monoclonal Antibodies

Basiliximab: Administer 12-mg doses using the same schedule as in adults

#### **Immunosuppression**

Baseline immunosuppression in pediatric patients mirrors that in adults, with a common strategy of steroid-free maintenance. Tacrolimus is the standard calcineurin inhibiter (Box 21.11). The use of induction therapy is highly variable among institutions. Overall, induction therapy with anti-lymphocyte antibody treatment (about 50%) or interleukin-2 receptor antagonist (about 25%) is standard practice, while about 25% of patients receive no induction.353

#### **Surveillance for Rejection**

In neonates and infants undergoing cardiac transplantation, repeated surveillance of cardiac biopsies may not be feasible because of limitations of vascular access. In this age group, echocardiographic variables have been identified that are generally predictive of acute rejection.354-356

In recent years, the technology to examine donor-specific cellfree DNA (cfDNA) has shown promise as a noninvasive marker for allograft rejection. In its current state of application, the greatest benefit appears to be as a screening tool, identifying patients who have a very low likelihood of rejection as diagnosed by EMB. A multicenter prospective study found that a donor fraction of cfDNA ,14% yielded a 96% probability of no treatable rejection by biopsy.357 These findings applied equally to pediatric as adult transplant recipients. If validated by further studies, this finding may offer a powerful screening tool for rejection in the pediatric population, potentially greatly reducing the number of biopsies required in pediatric patients.

#### **Infection Prophylaxis**

Prevention of infection by active immunization is recommended for specific conditions, using killed vaccine only.142 Recommended childhood immunizations are given in Table 21.16.

#### **Results**

#### **Survival**

*Survival by Era.* Among pediatric patients, survival has progressively improved over the past 3 decades, with an overall 1-year pediatric heart transplant survival of 92% during the period from 2012 through 2017 (Fig. 21.34).339,358 The improvement by era is evident for both cardiomyopathy (Fig. 21.35) and CHD (Fig. 21.36).339 Survival at 1 year is age-related. Based on the 2021 analysis from the International Heart and Lung Transplant Registry, 1-year survival was 89% among infants ,1 year, 92% among children age 1 to 10 years, and 94% among adolescents 11 to 17 years.358 Survival is the same as in adult transplant recipients once the patient reaches about 11 years of age. Of note, although early risk is somewhat greater for infants undergoing cardiac transplantation, the longer-term survival (beyond about 10 years) is highest among infant transplant recipients (Fig. 21.37).

*General Risk Factors.* A multivariable analysis of the ISHLT registry identified significant risk factors for mortality at 1 year: diagnosis of CHD, younger age (especially below 1 year), prior heart transplant, ECMO support at transplant, ventilator support, prior cardiac surgery, donor cause of death stroke versus trauma, female sex, decreased renal function, elevated serum bilirubin, higher PRA, longer donor heart ischemic time (especially . 5 hours), and center transplant volume (,20 pediatric heart transplants in prior 3 years) (Figs. 21.38 through 21.42). 358 Patients with CHD have approximately twice the risk of early mortality compared with cardiomyopathy patients; the 1-year survival by diagnosis in the era 2012 to 2017 was 95% for cardiomyopathy and 88% for CHD. Risk of early death appears to increase considerably and progressively with age less than about 1 year.

An analysis of a linkage between UNOS and the Pediatric Health Information System, a national administrative database, examined outcomes in 1517 patients transplanted with CHD*,*

*Donor Risk Factors.* The general risk factors identified in adults are applicable in children, with some refinements. A history of cardiopulmonary resuscitation (CPR), once considered a major risk factor, should not preclude heart transplantation. However, prolonged CPR (the duration has not been defined) or CPR followed by the need for high-dose inotropic support mitigate against safe transplantation.366 In pediatric donors, the need for inotropic support to support the circulation has not been associated with an increased risk of posttransplant graft dysfunction as long as function by echocardiographic assessment remains normal. However, the dosage or number of inotropes or vasopressors that are consistent with normal graft function remain unquantified, and caution is appropriate with multiple high dose inotropes that cannot be weaned prior to harvest. As in adults, abnormalities on electrocardiogram or troponin levels have not been predictive of graft dysfunction. The role of continuous warm or cold perfusion strategies has not been studied in pediatric donors. However, anecdotal cases support extensive study of this approach to expand the donor pool, including several cases of DCD using ex vivo perfusion.367-369

Risk Factors for Midterm and Late Mortality. Midterm and late survival, among patients alive at 1 year, are also affected by specific factors at the time of transplant.<sup>358</sup> Risk factors (all P < .01) for subsequent mortality within 5 years include retransplantation (HR 2.0), prior nontransplant cardiac surgery (HR 1.5), female recipient (HR 1.3), older and younger age (Fig. 21.43), and higher PRA at transplant. Among patients who survive the first posttransplant year, late survival is clearly superior in those transplanted during the first year of life (Fig. 21.44). The same trend toward improved late survival among infants is seen in the more recent data from PHTS (see Fig. 21.37). When considering late survival, infants have superior survival compared to other age groups, with a median survival of 25 years. The particularly favorable long-term survival among infants likely relates to relative immaturity of their immune system at the time of transplant.<sup>370</sup>

*Failing Fontan.* Historically, pediatric patients undergoing cardiac transplantation after a previous Fontan procedure have been considered at higher risk for mortality. However, recent analyses indicate similar survival to those with other forms of CHD (Fig. 21.45).<sup>371</sup> Analysis of a national US database in 2022 reported 89% and 74%
