To a separate contemporary analysis from the PHTS, including over 500 post-Fontan transplants, risk factors for posttransplant mortality among Fontan patients included worse liver and/or renal function (reflected in a high Model for End-Stage Liver Disease Xi score) (P=.02), history of protein-losing enteropathy (HR 2.1), and VAD at transplant (HR 3.4). The wever, another multiinstitutional study did not find an increased risk of posttransplant mortality among Fontan patients with plastic bronchitis, a more localized manifestation of protein-losing enteropathy. Requiring mechanical ventilation at transplant also appears to increase risk. The Both protein-losing enteropathy and plastic bronchitis usually resolve posttransplant.

Functional Single Ventricle. The results of heart transplantation in single ventricle physiology patients were compared with transplants for other CHD in a report from the Australia/New Zealand Registry, and no significant differences were found in early or late outcome. However, the median age of transplant was only 17 in this cohort. Tequivalent posttransplant survival between single and biventricular malformations was also reported from PHTS in a younger patient population (Fig. 21.47). Furthermore, similar survival is achieved with transplantation at each stage of palliation. Of note, early mortality is somewhat higher following heart transplantation at or before stage 1 palliation, but infants undergoing transplantation as primary therapy demonstrate superior late survival by about 10 years (Fig. 21.48). 339

Hypoplastic Left Heart Syndrome. Within the domain of functional single ventricle, hypoplastic left heart syndrome (HLHS) poses special challenges. The poor outcomes with any therapy for HLHS in the early 1980s provided the major opportunity to explore heart transplantation as primary therapy. The early excellent outcomes achieved at Loma Linda and elsewhere<sup>377</sup> with primary heart transplantation were accompanied by the challenges of inadequate donor heart availability and long waiting times in often unstable patients despite prostaglandins. After the pioneering advancements in surgical palliation of HLHS by Norwood,<sup>378</sup> the outcomes of first stage palliation were widely adopted by

![](_page_61_Figure_7.jpeg)

![](_page_61_Figure_8.jpeg)

• Figure 21.48 Survival after cardiac transplantation for single ventricle (SV), stratified by stage of palliation. PHTS 1993–2020; n= 2146. (From Richmond ME, Conway J, Kirklin JK, et al. Three decades of collaboration through the Pediatric Heart Transplant Society Registry: a journey through registry data with a highlight on children with single ventricle anatomy. Pediatr Transplant. 2024;28:e14615.)

![](_page_61_Figure_10.jpeg)

• Figure 21.49 Primary diagnosis for pediatric heart transplantation, US, 1993-2006. HLHS, hypoplastic left heart syndrome. (From unpublished data, Pediatric Heart Transplant Society, 2008.)

pediatric centers, with a progressive reduction in application of primary transplantation as definitive therapy (Fig. 21.49). By the end of the first decade of 2000, primary transplantation was reserved for those HLHS patients with either severe atrioventricular valve regurgitation or moderate or worse ventricular dysfunction.

Despite this evolution in practice, the long-term outcomes for primary transplantation for HLHS remain excellent. In a multicenter study from the Congenital Heart Surgeons' Society, patients receiving primary heart transplantation for aortic atresia between 1994 and 2000 had superior long-term survival (including waitlist mortality) compared to surgical palliation with the Norwood procedure (65% vs. 40% at 15 years; P = .002).  $^{379}$  Long-term survival after primary transplantation was superior to secondary transplantation after initial surgical palliation (74% vs. 48% at 9 years, P = .002). Primary transplant patients had a better functional outcome and equivalent quality of life compared to aortic atresia patients in the Fontan pathway. Multiinstitutional data indicate that posttransplant survival among patients with HLHS is currently similar to other forms of single ventricle (Fig. 21.50).  $^{371}$ 

![](_page_62_Figure_2.jpeg)

• Figure 21.50 Kaplan-Meier analysis demonstrating similar posttransplant survival of the three most common single ventricle diagnoses undergoing transplantation. HLHS, hypoplastic left heart syndrome; *Tricusp*, tricuspid; *V*, ventricle; *Yr*, year. (From Richmond ME, Conway J, Kirklin JK, et al. Three decades of collaboration through the Pediatric Heart Transplant Society Registry: a journey through registry data with a highlight on children with single ventricle anatomy. *Pediatr Transplant*. 2024;28:e14615.)

Heterotaxy Syndrome. Among patients with CHD, heterotaxy presents multiple challenges with transplantation. About 70% of heterotaxy patients undergoing heart transplantation have single ventricle physiology. An analysis from PHTS identified heterotaxy syndrome as an independent risk factor for mortality among patients with CHD, both early and later following heart transplantation. The early incremental risk has been neutralized in the most recent era. Within the heterotaxy cohort, single ventricle physiology did not confer an added risk posttransplant.

From a surgical perspective, the frequent presence of interrupted IVC with azygous continuation in polysplenia cases represents an important surgical challenge in reconstruction of systemic venous return. Historically, polysplenia has been a risk factor for early posttransplant mortality. <sup>380</sup> In the most recent era, this risk has been neutralized, likely through increased surgical experience with reconstructive techniques (see section on "Technique of Operation") and improved imaging of the complex venous anatomy.

Compared to other forms of CHD, heterotaxy patients experience lower freedom from posttransplant infection. This may in part relate to the known propensity among heterotaxy patients for respiratory infections, possibly linked to primary ciliary dysfunction and (among asplenia patients) greater susceptibility to infections with encapsulated bacteria. 381,382

Adults with Congenital Heart Diseases. Among adult patients with congenital heart disease (ACHD), early mortality has been consistently higher compared to those without CHD. Mortality risk is doubled in the first year after transplantation if the indication has been adult CHD.<sup>44</sup> The long wait times and paucity of useful mechanical support devices in this group probably contribute to an increased early mortality. However, the longer-term outcomes are reasonably good, with 10-year survival rates equivalent to or better than noncongenital patients. Over time, as demonstrated by a propensity matched analysis of US UNOS transplants (at age >18 years) between 2000 and 2020, the survival difference becomes minimal.<sup>383</sup> The overall unmatched survival at 1 year was 85% for ACHD patients and 93% for

non-ACHD patients (P < .001). At 18 years, the comparative survivals were 40% (ACHD) versus 30% (non-ACHD). Among propensity matched patients, the survival difference at 10 years was 71% (ACHD) versus 75% (non-ACHD) (P < .001) and by 18 years, the survival was equivalent at about 40% in each group. Thus, in the current era, adults transplanted with CHD can expect essentially equivalent longer-term survival as other diagnostic cohorts. Risk factors for poor outcomes include single ventricle anatomy, anatomic complexity, protein losing enteropathy, and high titers of panel reactive antibodies.  $^{384}$ 

Despite the increasing experience with transplantation for adult congenital heart disease, limited data are known on Fontan adults. Looking at combined pediatric and adult series in recent years shows that transplantation of Fontan adults over age 25 is rarer than one would expect.<sup>376</sup> Thus, risk factors and outcome data are largely unavailable on the older Fontan undergoing transplantation. However, limited adult experiences underscore the importance of long-standing elevated venous pressures on liver and kidneys with resultant posttransplant complications and mortality. Success with combined heart and liver transplants is now being recorded from some major centers in small numbers, but the indications remain controversial (see "Special Situations and Controversies").

#### Rejection

Among pediatric patients, approximately 22% develop clinical rejection during the first year, and nearly 75% of rejection episodes occur during the first 3 months. Among infants, rejection is increasingly diagnosed without EMB, but among older children and adolescents, over 75% of episodes are identified by EMB. The use of peripheral blood biomarkers such as cell-free DNA are under active investigation in adult cardiac recipients, but these have not yet been extensively studied in children.

The use of induction therapy (most commonly an interleukin-2 receptor antagonist [IL-2RA]) is associated with decreased rejection. A multiinstitutional study from PHTS supports the protective effect of IL-2RA, particularly in lower-risk patients, in which both infection and rejection were reduced, but without a clear survival advantage. 353

Patients who develop rejection during the first year have a much higher likelihood (>10-fold increase) of developing rejection during the subsequent 2 years compared to those without rejection in the first year. Thus, if pediatric patients are diagnosed with rejection in the first year, continued close surveillance for rejection, possibly with EMB, should be considered.

#### Infection

Infection risk and sites of involvement after pediatric heart transplantation generally mirror the adult transplant experience (see Section I). Bacterial infections are the most common type, and the risk of infection is highest during the first month. Blood-stream infections account for 25% of early infections. Coagulasenegative staphylococci (17%), Enterobacter sp (11.99%), and Pseudomonas sp (11.62%) are the most common organisms. Multidrug resistance is common. Risk factors for early bacterial infection include younger age (P < .0001), ventilator at transplant (P < .0001), and extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (P = .03). at transplant. Mortality after early major bacterial infection exceeds 30% and is higher with prior cardiac surgery and multiple sites (P < .001).

Patients transplanted following the Fontan operation are at particular risk for infection, particularly the immunophenotypes with lymphopenia and/or protein-losing enteropathy. In a PHTS study, the freedom from infection during the first year was 22% with either of these conditions versus 41% without (*P* , .001).340

#### **Allograft Vasculopathy**

In contrast to adults, vascular access (usually femoral artery) for yearly or frequent coronary angiography in children represents an important ongoing risk for vascular occlusion. Thus, protocols for surveillance methods for CAV vary widely among centers. Furthermore, the yield from individual studies is low. A multicenter study from PHTS found that 97% of pediatric coronary evaluations were for routine surveillance, with detection of CAV in ,5%.388 Approximately 60% of centers continue to use only coronary angiography for surveillance, whereas 40% use supplementary noninvasive testing for surveillance. Among patients with suggestive symptoms, the yield from angiography is much higher (about 30%).

Among pediatric recipients, freedom from CAV is 90% at 5 years and 75% at 10 years.389 Older age at transplant confers a somewhat greater risk for early CAV (,3 years posttransplant) with a mean transplant age of 8 years for early CAV versus 4 years for late CAV (.10 years posttransplant) (*P* , .0001). Graft survival following detection of CAV is 60% to 70% at 5 years and 30% to 40% at 10 years, considerably worse than without CAV. The risk of subsequent graft loss is similar with detection anytime during the first 10 years posttransplant but is somewhat better with late onset CAV (.10 years posttransplant) (HR 0.63, *P* 5 .04). Among pediatric recipients, risk factors for CAV include retransplantation (HR 1.5, *P* 5 .01), prior cardiac surgery (HR 1.3, *P* 5 .01), female sex (HR 1.3, *P* , .01),358 obesity (HR 1.2, *P* 5 .04), and dyslipidemia (increased total cholesterol, LDL cholesterol, and triglycerides) (HR 1.5-1.8, *P* , .0001).390

In contrast to adult heart transplant recipients, who frequently have an accelerated atherosclerotic component superimposed on the immunologic basis for CAV (see Section I, "Allograft Vasculopathy"), the atherosclerotic component is less prominent in pediatric patients. Although aspirin is often prescribed as preventive therapy for CAV in pediatric patients, a large multiinstitutional analysis failed to demonstrate a survival or freedom from CAV advantage.391 Similarly, the beneficial effects of statin therapy on adult CAV294 have not been reproduced in pediatric recipients.392

#### **Malignancy**

Malignancy, including PTLD, is discussed in detail in the section on "Malignancy" in Section I ("Cardiac Transplantation in Adults without Congenital Heart Disease"). The prevalence of posttransplant malignancy in children is 5% to 10% among 10-year survivors, and lymphoma accounts for practically all cases in this age range.393 The most frequent malignancy in pediatric patients is PTLD, which is frequently triggered by EBV and is typically of B-cell origin. The risk of PTLD was highest in children between the ages of 1 and 10. Malignancy in general was the main cause of mortality in 4% of juvenile heart recipients who lived above the age of 10. PTLD occurs in 5% to 15% of pediatric recipients within 3 years posttransplant.394 An additional 3% of patients develop PTLD after 3 years. Risk factors for development of late PTLD include absence of EBV antibodies (HR 1.65, *P* 5 0.02) and younger age (HR 1.06, *P* 5 .003). Survival after late PTLD is 86% at 1 year and 68% at 5 years. Rejection and infection are common after late PTLD, occurring in 25% to 35% of patients. Risk factors for mortality include non-white race (HR 2.27, *P* 5 .03) and earlier year of heart transplant (HR 1.03, *P* 5 .04). A diagnosis of CHD is also a risk factor for the development of PTLD, especially with prior cardiac surgery, in which thymectomy (often performed at initial operation to improve access to the heart) at a young age is associated with alteration in T lymphocyte subsets that may contribute to an impaired immune response to EBV infection.395 A study of the PHTS registry failed to find any correlation between the risk of PTLD in children and induction therapy.353

#### **Cardiac Retransplantation**

Given the goal of survival well into adulthood, retransplantation is an integral part of pediatric transplant therapy. Cardiac retransplants account for ,5% of pediatric heart transplants.396 Among pediatric patients ,18 years at primary transplant, survival after retransplantation has improved over the past decade, but remains inferior to primary transplant (median survival 9.3 vs. 20.2 years, *P* , .001). By multivariable analysis, risk factors for subsequent graft loss (all *P* , .01) include ECMO support at retransplant (HR 10.1), MCS at retransplant (HR 7.5), duration of first transplant ,1 year (HR 5.3), and diagnosis of CHD (HR 4.4). For each 1-year increase in duration of first transplant, the survival after retransplantation increases by about 15%. Patients after retransplantation generally experience more rejection, infection, and allograft vasculopathy (all *P* , .05). Retransplantation for acute graft failure and acute rejection with hemodynamic compromise are known to have poor outcomes; this therapy should be rarely applied in those situations.

#### **Functional Outcomes and Quality of Life**

Cardiac transplantation presents major barriers to functional and emotional development, related both to the underlying cardiac illness and to the immunosuppressive regimen and need for ongoing medical follow-up. A study of nearly 1500 older children and adolescents reported that at the time of listing, only 12% required modified educational placement.397 Those with CHD, nonprivate insurance, and those with prolonged hospitalization were most likely to require modified educational placement. The proportion of patients with normal educational placement remained constant at about 87% at 3 and 5 years posttransplant. In addition to the factors at listing, posttransplant infections were a major contributor to the need for modified education placement.

## **Indications for Pediatric Cardiac Transplantation**

Pretransplantation evaluation is multidisciplinary, similar to that for any other heart failure patient. Pulmonary vascular resistance (Rp) may be elevated in any patient with heart failure; however, it is more commonly encountered when there is a history of longstanding CHD in addition to heart failure, particularly if the CHD is associated with left-to-right shunting. If there is no evidence of intracardiac or arterial-level shunting, Rp-related contraindications to heart transplantation in the adult with CHD are similar to those for any patient and include Rp greater than or equal to 6 WU or a transpulmonary gradient above 15 mmHg that is unresponsive to pulmonary vasodilator therapy in the catheterization laboratory.44,45 Transpulmonary gradient alone is less helpful in the presence of either increased or decreased , which is commonly encountered in many forms of uncorrected CHD. In addition, magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) or CT of the chest may be helpful in defining extracardiac morphology, such as systemic venous anomalies, arch anomalies, and the relationship of the aorta, pulmonary trunk, conduit (if present), or ventricular mass to the posterior table of the sternum (see Chapter 5).

More than 80% of cardiac conditions leading to cardiac transplantation in the pediatric population are either cardiomyopathies or CHD, with CHD not amenable to correction or palliation now comprising approximately 40% of all pediatric listings for transplantation.398

The congenital cardiac malformations considered for cardiac transplantation range from unrepaired complex disease in the newborn to repaired or palliated CHD with ventricular dysfunction in the adult.399-403 Cardiac transplantation is currently not recommended as routine primary therapy for any congenital cardiac malformation, but severe coronary anomalies, cardiac valve regurgitation, or ventricular dysfunction can favor primary transplantation or referral after initial palliation in selected infants with complex CHD, particularly among patients with functional single ventricle. In the current era, 50% of pediatric patients listed for heart transplantation have a history of prior cardiac surgery.339

Among infants with hypoplastic left heart syndrome, referral for cardiac transplantation should be considered when right ventricular dysfunction or tricuspid regurgitation, or both, are severe either prior to or following initial surgical palliation. In older children and adolescents, failure of staged single-ventricle palliation is the most common form of CHD referred for cardiac transplantation (Table 21.17). Patients with moderate to severe atrioventricular valve regurgitation or systemic ventricular dysfunction prior to the second stage (bidirectional Glenn) are at greatest risk for failure to achieve successful Fontan and can be considered for cardiac transplantation.404

The relatively small number of pediatric patients who are referred for cardiac transplantation, and the variety of conditions that can progress to cardiac failure, have limited the ability to generate specific guidelines for transplant referrals (Box 21.12). Recent consensus guidelines from the American Heart Association405 include the following recommendations for patients with cardiomyopathies or previously repaired CHD (these guidelines do not address functional single ventricle):

- A need for ongoing IV inotropic or MCS for poor ventricular function and low cardiac output
- Progressive deterioration of ventricular function or functional status despite optimal medical care with digitalis, diuretics, ACE inhibitors, and b-blockade
- Malignant arrhythmias or survival after arrhythmia-induced cardiac arrest unresponsive to medical treatment, catheter ablation, or an implantable cardioverter-defibrillator
- Restrictive cardiomyopathy associated with reactive pulmonary hypertension
- Progressive pulmonary hypertension secondary to systemic ventricular failure that could preclude cardiac transplantation at a later date
- Growth failure secondary to severe heart failure unresponsive to conventional medical treatment
- Unacceptably poor quality of life secondary to heart failure

#### **• BOX 21.12 Guidelines for Pediatric Cardiac Transplant Referral**

- Complex congenital cardiac anomaly with progressive heart failure not amenable to conventional repair or palliation
- Infants, children, and adolescents: progressive deterioration in left ventricular ejection fraction or functional status despite optimal medical therapy, failure to grow secondary to advanced heart failure symptoms, or a progressive rise in pulmonary vascular resistance that would be expected to preclude transplantation at a later date

Modified from Renlund DG, Taylor DO. Cardiac transplantation. In: Topol EJ, ed. *Textbook of Cardiovascular Medicine.* 2nd ed. Philadelphia: Lippincott Williams & Wilkins; 2002.

- Progressive deterioration in functional status or presence of certain high-risk conditions following the Fontan procedure Noncardiac contraindications to transplantation in CHD are similar to those for transplantation in acquired cardiac disease (see Section I). Certain situations specific to pediatric patients contraindicate cardiac transplantation, as recommended by the American Heart Association Council on Cardiovascular Disease in the Young405:
- Heart transplantation for pediatric heart disease is not efficacious when heart disease is associated with severe irreversible disease in other organ systems, or when it is part of a severe irreversible multisystemic disease process. Multiorgan transplantation may be considered.
- Orthotopic heart transplantation for pediatric heart disease is not efficacious when heart disease is associated with severe irreversible fixed elevation of pulmonary vascular resistance.
- Heart transplantation is not feasible in the presence of severe hypoplasia of the central pulmonary arteries or pulmonary veins.

• The limited supply of pediatric donors, especially infant donors, makes heart transplantation not a feasible standard therapy for any specific congenital heart lesion.

#### **Special Situations and Controversies**

#### **ABO-Incompatible Heart Transplants**

Infants in the first few months of life manifest a deficiency in certain types of B-cell responses. Although such responses to protein antigen stimulation are generally competent early in life, antibody production to T-independent carbohydrate antigens is notably deficient.406,407 This finding is particularly relevant to development of natural antibodies directed against nonself A or B blood group antigens. Available evidence suggests that these natural ABO antibodies develop as a cross-reaction to similar carbohydrate epitopes on intestinal *Escherichia coli* in normal gut flora.408 This deficiency in the ability to produce antibodies against carbohydrate antigens is manifest during early infancy by the absence of natural anti-A or anti-B blood group antibodies (isohemagglutinins) until approximately age 4 to 6 months.406

The paucity of available donors for neonates and small infants requiring cardiac transplantation and this relative neonatal immune deficiency stimulated West and colleagues in Toronto to consider the possibility of ABO-incompatible (ABOi) cardiac transplantation to expand the donor pool for neonatal transplantation.407 In 2001, they reported successful application of this strategy in six infants who received ABOi hearts.409 Most underwent transplantation before onset of isohemagglutinin anti-A or anti-B antibody production. Standard immunosuppressive strategies were used, and hyperacute rejection was not observed. For those patients in whom anti-ABO antibodies were detected, plasma exchange was performed during CPB.410

Over the past 2 decades, ABOi heart transplantation has become routine among pediatric patients under 2 years of age.411,412 Monitoring of anti-A/B isohemagglutinins is performed at intervals with a target of no detectable antibodies at a dilution of #1:16. Accumulation of hemagglutinin titers following transplant is prevented by incorporating plasma exchange (typically two to threefold volume exchange) in the immediate pretransplant period, often prior to initiating CPB, with a maximum threshold of 1:2. An alternative method of antibody reduction utilizes intraoperative immunoabsorption during CPB prior to organ implant, with incorporation of an anti-A/B immunoadsorption column into the CPB circuit.413 An initial experience indicates equivalent hemagglutinin titers, survival, and freedom from rejection, with less blood product utilization.

A recent analysis from the PHTS noted an increase in the percent of patients ,2 years of age listed for ABOi hearts from 49% in 2010 to 72% in 2017.414 As of 2020, 20% of transplant patients ,2 years of age are ABOi. Time to transplant was significantly reduced among patients listed for ABOi donors, especially for blood group O recipients (*P* , .02). Posttransplant survival out to 5 years was similar between ABOi and ABO compatible (ABOc) transplants except for patients who were initially listed for ABOc and transplanted with ABOi donors. This subset had a higher risk profile (reflecting more urgent transplants), and after matching for risk factors, the survival difference was no longer present. The safety of properly performed ABOi heart transplantation is also supported by the similar freedom from rejection, allograft vasculopathy, and malignancy compared to ABOc transplants.415 Of interest, freedom from bacterial infections with encapsulated bacteria was better with ABOi transplants, suggesting a possible favorable effect of the acquired immunological changes associated with the ABO tolerance of ABOi transplantation.

#### **Heart Versus Heart-Liver Transplant for Failing Fontan with Fontan-Associated Liver Disease**

In the presence of advanced Fontan-associated liver disease and evidence of cirrhosis in the post-Fontan patient considered for heart transplantation, controversy exists whether heart-only or combined heart-liver transplantation offers the best chance for mid-term survival. Some degree of hepatic fibrosis is common late after the Fontan operation, and fibrosis is usually heterogeneous. Therefore, liver biopsy is prone to sampling error and may underestimate extent of disease. Liver function studies often lag behind severity of disease. Findings on imaging studies (ultrasound, CT scan, and MRI) suggestive of cirrhosis include liver surface nodularity, portal vein dilation, portosystemic collaterals, abnormal portal vein flow patterns, and ascites.416 Even with documented evidence of extensive fibrosis, precise indications for heart-liver transplant have not been established. Several studies suggest equivalent survival with heart alone or heart-liver transplantation.417-420 However, it should be noted that cirrhosis on imaging studies is associated with worse outcomes with heart-only transplantation.421 A single center study reported on 14 adult Fontan (14 to 31 years duration of Fontan) patients undergoing heartonly transplantation.422 Among 10 patients surviving .1 year, 8 had ultrasound evidence of cirrhosis at transplant. The liver appearance by ultrasound remained abnormal out to .5 years posttransplant but without significant liver disease progression.

#### **COVID-19 Infection**

During the 2020–2023 SARS-CoV-2 coronavirus pandemic, pediatric heart transplant patients were expected to be more susceptible to complications related to COVID-19. Studies to date indicate a high infection incidence, in as high as 20% of posttransplant patients.423 About half the identified patients (RNA PCR testing) remained asymptomatic. The suppressed immune system was not a major barrier to postinfection antibody response, with seroconversion documented in 70% of those tested. A multicenter analysis from PHTS examined the prevalence and course of COVID-19 among patients listed and following transplantation in the period from April of 2020 to March 2022.424 Four percent of transplant candidates and 5% of transplanted patients (179/3831) developed COVID-19. A positive PCR test was documented in .90% of cases. Hospitalization was common, occurring in 40% of pretransplant COVID patients and 21% of those transplanted. Intensive unit care was required in 20% of pretransplant patients and 7% of transplant recipients. The need for mechanical ventilation was uncommon, occurring in 10% of those pretransplant and 1% of transplant patients. Ninety-five percent of patients had resolution of symptoms at 30 days, and mortality was rare (1 patient) within 30 days of diagnosis.

#### **SECTION III: HEART-LUNG TRANSPLANTATION**

#### **Historical Note**

The first combined heart-lung transplant procedure was performed by Denton Cooley in Texas on September 15, 1969. The patient was a 2-month-old infant who survived only 14 hours. C. Walton Lillehei performed the same procedure in an adult patient in New York 3 months later, and that patient survived 8 days. After a sustained experimental investigation of heartlung transplantation, Bruce Reitz and colleagues at Stanford performed the first successful combined procedure in 1981. Long-term survival became possible with the availability of cyclosporine, and the first successful series of patients undergoing heart-lung transplantation was published by Reitz and colleagues the following year.425

#### **Clinical Features**

#### **Recipient Evaluation and Selection**

All the standard recipient selection criteria for isolated heart transplantation apply to patients considered for heart-lung transplantation. However, certain issues unique to this therapy require special mention. Because a heart-lung block is used that could otherwise potentially benefit three individuals with advanced heart or lung failure, there is an added responsibility to select heart-lung transplant recipients with sufficient overall reserves to both benefit from the procedure and have a good probability for midterm survival. In addition, the magnitude of heart-lung transplantation and the associated overall stress on other subsystems are, in most instances, greater than those for isolated heart or lung transplantation.

*Age.* A clear relationship between death after heart-lung transplantation and older age has not been formally demonstrated, but recipients older than age 60 are rarely selected.

*Previous Thoracic Surgery.* Major bleeding from adhesions in a previously operated thoracic space is a strong risk factor for morbidity and mortality after heart-lung transplantation. This in part relates to major transfusion requirements, potentially destabilizing effects of ongoing bleeding on cardiac function immediately after transplantation, and difficulties in identifying and preserving the phrenic nerves in the presence of dense adhesions. As a result, some centers decline patients for heart-lung transplantation if they have undergone prior thoracotomy, particularly if bilateral. With experience, patients with prior thoracotomy and sternotomy can safely undergo heart-lung transplantation (see "Technique of Operation"), but they should have well-preserved noncardiac organ function and be younger, generally less than age 50 years.

## **Technique of Operation**

#### **Preoperative and Intraoperation Preparation**

Preoperative preparation for heart-lung transplantation is the same as for orthotopic heart transplantation. In the setting of no prior cardiothoracic operation, the procedure is performed through a median sternotomy. However, when one or both pleural spaces have been previously entered, a bilateral anterior thoracotomy is preferred as in the technique of bilateral lung transplantation. This allows safer dissection and control of bleeding from adhesions in both pleural spaces. A pericardial flap is developed by incising the pericardium laterally and inferiorly, leaving about 5 cm of pericardium over the SVC as the base for an autologous pericardial flap to be placed around the tracheal anastomosis. Cautery must be avoided in the vicinity of either phrenic nerve, because heat-transmitted injury would catastrophically impair postoperative pulmonary function.

#### **Removal of Native Heart and Lungs**

CPB is established, the aorta clamped, and a standard cardiectomy performed as for bicaval orthotopic cardiac transplantation (see "Orthotopic Cardiac Transplantation" under "Technique of Operation" in Section I). If pulmonary venous return is excessive, appropriate reduction of perfusate temperature to profound hyperthermic levels is instituted to allow intermittent low-flow perfusion to facilitate dissection and avoid excessive blood loss (see Section IV of Chapter 2). Otherwise, perfusate temperature is generally 28°C.

Beginning on the left side, an incision is made in the pericardium just anterior to the pulmonary veins as far below the phrenic nerve as possible. In the absence of dense adhesions, a pedicle of pericardium containing the phrenic nerve is developed inferiorly almost to the diaphragm and superiorly just to the level of the left pulmonary artery to avoid damaging the phrenic nerve. The left atrium is divided posteriorly in its midportion, and a cuff of left atrium along with the left pulmonary veins is dissected free and passed under the pericardial pedicle (Fig. 21.51A). The left pulmonary artery is dissected circumferentially proximal to its branches and divided. The lower lobe of the left lung is reflected superiorly, and the inferior pulmonary ligament is divided with cautery. The posterior pulmonary attachments are divided, and the lung is mobilized away from the vagus nerve. The left mainstem bronchus is isolated by dividing lymphatic tissue with cautery and hemoclips, and the bronchus is stapled and divided. The left lung is passed off the surgical field.

Similarly, the pericardium on the right side is dissected immediately above the pulmonary vein, and the pericardial pedicle is mobilized. The inferior pulmonary ligament is mobilized, right pulmonary artery dissected circumferentially and divided, and posterior pulmonary attachments divided. The lung is gently freed from the area of the vagus nerve without cautery, and the right mainstem bronchus is isolated, stapled, and divided. After removing both lungs, stumps of right and left pulmonary arteries are mobilized and the pulmonary trunk excised, leaving a generous cuff around the area of ligamentum arteriosum and left recurrent laryngeal nerve. The bronchial stumps are mobilized to the level of the carina with careful hemostasis of bronchial arteries (Fig. 21.51B). Complete hemostasis is critical to achieve throughout the pleural space and mediastinum before implanting the heart-lung block.

#### **Implantation of the Heart-Lung Block**

The heart-lung block is prepared on a back table by dividing the donor trachea approximately 2 cm above the carina. It is important to leave the membranous portion slightly longer than the cartilaginous portion, because it tends to retract slightly after division. The anatomic position of each lung is beneath the pericardial pedicle on each side, but in the presence of dense adhesions from prior operation, both lungs can safely be placed anterior to the phrenic nerves, which avoids additional dissection. In that instance, the pulmonary veins are divided outside the pericardium leaving the intrapericardial pulmonary veins and posterior left atrial wall intact. This may, however, require additional mobilization of tissue anterior to the phrenic nerves to allow the lungs to sit nicely in each pleural space.

The tracheal anastomosis is facilitated by placing silk stay sutures on the donor trachea after dividing it just above the carina. It is important to avoid any additional dissection superior to the

![](_page_67_Picture_2.jpeg)

point of division to preserve all possible blood supply to the tracheal anastomosis. The anastomosis is constructed with continuous 3-0 polypropylene sutures in adults and 4-0 polypropylene sutures in children (Fig. 21.51C). An air-tight anastomosis must be achieved without necrosis of the membranous portion and without turning excessive amounts of tissue into the tracheal lumen. The pericardium is wrapped around the trachea and sutured to the thick lymphatic tissue anterior to the suture line. Gentle inflation of the lung while immersing the tracheal anastomosis in saline allows validation of an air-tight suture line. During implantation of the heart-lung block, the organs are covered with ice slush to maintain topical hypothermia.

The inferior and superior vena caval and aortic anastomoses are constructed, as in the bicaval orthotopic heart transplant technique (see "Orthotopic Cardiac Transplantation" under "Technique of Operation" in Section I). During rewarming, after suture lines are completed, the lungs are gently ventilated, and CPB is discontinued as usual. Nitric oxide is used in the presence of any reperfusion-induced pulmonary hypertension.

## **Special Features of Postoperative Care**

Posttransplant management is similar to that for heart transplantation, except for additional protocols to enhance pulmonary graft function. Fiberoptic bronchoscopy is routinely performed in the operating room at the end of the procedure to clear any secretions and examine the tracheal anastomosis. It should be performed again just prior to extubation and as indicated thereafter. Because of potential ischemia-reperfusion injury and lymphatic disruption, a reimplantation response—evidenced by pulmonary perihilar haziness on chest radiograph and increased oxygen requirement—is common 2 to 5 days following heart-lung transplantation. Diuresis and fluid restriction is the primary therapy. When this process persists for 1 week or more, acute rejection should be considered, with transbronchial biopsy assessment of suspected rejection.

Current immunosuppression protocols for heart-lung transplantation are similar to those for isolated heart transplantation with the exception of the steroid protocol (see Table 21.4). Although unproven, higher-dose prednisone is considered deleterious to tracheal healing, and oral prednisone is initiated at a dose of about 0.4 mgper kg/day with a rapid taper. Surveillance for and management of lung rejection includes the same protocols that have been established for isolated single and double lung transplantation.

#### **Results**

#### **Survival**

Since the first successful combined heart-lung transplant (HLT) by Reitz and colleagues at Stanford in 1981, over 4000 HLTs have been performed worldwide.425,426 However, the evolution of thoracic transplantation has moved away from combined heart-lung transplantation except in a small number of centers for the unique indications mentioned later. In the United States, the number of heart-lung transplants is currently ,50 per year.426

Survival is generally inferior to isolated heart or lung transplantation, with a 1-year survival of approximately 80% and 10-year survival of 30% to 40%. This is largely attributed to higher early mortality, likely related to the complexity of the operation. However, among survivors at 1 year, subsequent survival mirrors that of isolated double lung transplantation (DLT) (median conditional survival after 1 year is 10.0 years for HLT vs. 9.7 for DLT), both of which are inferior to isolated heart transplantation (13.0 years).426 Patients with HLT for CHD (with or without Eisenmenger syndrome) have a conditional median survival of 11.3 and 13.3 years. Early graft failure is the most common cause of death in the first month, and infection remains the major cause of mortality thereafter, accounting for approximately one-third of deaths after the first month. As in isolated lung transplantation, bronchiolitis obliterans accounts for 20% to 40% of mortality observed after the first year.

### **Rejection and Allograft Vasculopathy**

Cardiac allograft rejection is less common with combined HLT than in isolated heart transplants.426 Therefore, most centers employ a less frequent schedule of surveillance EMB. Similarly, allograft vasculopathy appears to be less common with combined HLT. The International Society for Heart and Lung Transplantation reported the incidence of allograft vasculopathy at 1 and 5 years posttransplant at 7.7% and 30% after HT versus 2.7% and 9% after HLT.428 The observed reduction in acute and chronic rejection with combined heart and lung transplants has been hypothesized to relate to the large amount of lymphoid tissue in the lungs acting to dominate the immune response, serving as protection for the heart against immune-mediated injury.

In contrast, lung rejection appears similar to that with isolated lung transplantation, and similar surveillance protocols are followed.

#### **Indications for Heart-Lung Transplantation**

The current general indications for heart-lung transplantation are (1) Eisenmenger syndrome with an uncorrected intracardiac defect, (2) uncorrectable CHD with atresia or diffuse severe hypoplasia of the pulmonary arteries and progressive heart failure, (3) coexistence of severe cardiopulmonary disease with advanced heart or lung failure, and (4) severe heart failure with left ventricular dysfunction and secondary pulmonary hypertension unresponsive to vasodilator therapy.

In the current era of limited donor availability combined with the success achieved in single and double lung transplantation, isolated lung transplantation is the preferred therapy for advanced lung failure associated with emphysema, usual interstitial pneumonitis, and suppurative lung disease, including cystic fibrosis. Primary pulmonary hypertension refractory to medical therapy is currently preferentially treated with bilateral lung transplantation instead of combined heart-lung transplantation.

The natural history of Eisenmenger syndrome—characterized by excessive muscularization of the pulmonary vasculature that results in marked irreversible elevation of pulmonary vascular resistance and reversal of a previously left-to-right shunt at the atrial, ventricular, or aortopulmonary level, resulting in right-toleft shunting and cyanosis—has not been characterized well enough to allow data-driven decisions regarding timing of referral for heart-lung transplantation. The time course of deterioration in this condition is slow, with 25-year survival exceeding 40%.429 In general, the timing of listing for heart-lung transplantation is based on:

- Worsening cyanosis
- Right ventricular failure—particularly with marked ascites and peripheral edema—that is unresponsive to diuretic therapy
- Worsening oxygen saturation, particularly once it reaches 60% with exertion
- Progressive fatigue and dyspnea, particularly if repeated phlebotomy is required
